=== rds-apply-immediately-vs-window | rds | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=3.3.K4
C: Modifications not applied immediately go into the pending modifications queue and are applied in the next maintenance window; choosing Apply Immediately applies the new change and everything already in the queue.
Q: What does Apply Immediately do compared with waiting for the maintenance window?
V: v1=pending modifications queue || v2=pending changes in the queue
S: scope='DB instance modifications' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/USER_ModifyInstance.ApplyImmediately] If you don't choose to apply changes immediately, RDS puts the changes into the pending modifications queue. During the next maintenance window, RDS applies any pending changes in the queue. If you choose to apply changes immediately, your new changes and any changes in the pending modifications queue are applied.

=== rds-backup-storage-per-region-s3 | rds | behavior | hard=False | extended | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=4.3.S1,4.3.S2
C: RDS backup storage is counted per Region as the automated backups plus manual DB snapshots in that Region; copying a snapshot to another Region adds to that Region's backup storage; backups are stored in Amazon S3.
Q: Where are RDS backups stored, and how is backup storage counted?
V: v1=Backups are stored in Amazon S3. || v2=for each AWS Region
S: scope='per AWS Region' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/USER_WorkingWithAutomatedBackups] Your Amazon RDS backup storage for each AWS Region is composed of the automated backups and manual DB snapshots for that Region. Total backup storage space equals the sum of the storage for all backups in that Region. Moving a DB snapshot to another Region increases the backup storage in the destination Region. Backups are stored in Amazon S3.

This operation can take several hours to complete Any data updates that occurred after the latest restorable time (typically within the last 5 minutes) will not be available | A new primary is available to serve your new workload in as quickly as 60 seconds Failover time is independent of write throughput | A new primary is available to serve your new workload in typically under 35 seconds Failover time depends on length of replica lag |

S: scope='cross-Region read replicas' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/USER_ReadRepl.XRgn] You can expect to see a higher level of lag time for any read replica that is in a different AWS Region than the source instance. This lag time comes from the longer network channels between regional data centers.
=== rds-cross-region-replica-source-deleted | rds | behavior | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=3.3.K7
C: When the source of a cross-Region read replica is deleted, the replica is promoted for Db2, MariaDB, SQL Server, MySQL and Oracle; for PostgreSQL its replication status becomes terminated and it is not promoted.
Q: What happens to a cross-Region read replica when its source DB instance is deleted?
V: v1=the read replica is promoted || v2=The read replica isn't promoted.
S: scope='cross-Region read replicas, per engine' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/USER_ReadRepl.XRgn] For Db2, MariaDB, Microsoft SQL Server, MySQL, and Oracle DB instances, when the source DB instance for a cross-Region read replica is deleted, the read replica is promoted.
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/USER_ReadRepl.XRgn] For PostgreSQL DB instances, when the source DB instance for a cross-Region read replica is deleted, the replication status of the read replica is set to `terminated`. The read replica isn't promoted.

=== rds-custom-vs-rds-vs-ec2 | rds | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=3.3.K6,3.3.S2
C: AWS recommends Amazon RDS when the database and OS should be fully managed by AWS, RDS Custom when you need administrative rights to the database and underlying OS, and self-managed databases on Amazon EC2 when you want full management responsibility.
Q: How do you choose between Amazon RDS, RDS Custom and a database on EC2?
V: v1=Amazon RDS Custom is the better choice || v2=self-managing your commercial databases on Amazon EC2
S: scope='Amazon RDS Custom' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/rds-custom] If you need the entire database and operating system to be fully managed by AWS, we recommend Amazon RDS. If you need administrative rights to the database and underlying operating system to make dependent applications available, Amazon RDS Custom is the better choice. If you want full management responsibility and simply need a managed compute service, the best option is self-managing your commercial databases on Amazon EC2.

=== rds-faq-blue-green-switchover-blocks-writes | rds | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-26-faq | bul=3.3.S2
C: A Blue/Green switchover blocks writes to both environments until it completes, lets green catch up, redirects traffic to green, and only then enables writes on green, so no data is lost.
Q: How does an RDS Blue/Green Deployments switchover work?
V: block-writes=block writes || zero-data-loss=zero data loss
S: scope='RDS Blue/Green Deployments' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/rds/faqs] When Amazon RDS Blue/Green Deployments initiate a switchover, they block writes to both the blue and green environments, until switchover is complete. During switchover, the staging environment, or green environment, catches up with the production system, ensuring data is consistent between the staging and production environment.
E: [www/rds/faqs] Blue/Green Deployments are designed to enable writes on the green environment after switchover is complete, ensuring zero data loss during the switchover process.
=== rds-faq-blue-green-use-cases | rds | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-26-faq | bul=3.3.S2
C: Blue/Green Deployments suit major or minor engine version upgrades, operating system updates, schema changes that do not break logical replication, and parameter changes, with short, predictable downtime.
Q: When should you use Amazon RDS Blue/Green Deployments?
V: major-or-minor-version-database-engine-upgrades=major or minor version database engine upgrades || short-predictable-downtime=short, predictable downtime
S: scope='RDS Blue/Green Deployments' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/rds/faqs] Blue/Green Deployments are ideal for use cases such as major or minor version database engine upgrades, operating system updates, schema changes on green environments that do not break logical replication, like adding a new column at the end of a table, or database parameter setting changes.
E: [www/rds/faqs] This allows you to stay current on security patches, improve database performance, and access newer database features with short, predictable downtime.

=== rds-faq-multi-az-for-write-availability | rds | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-26-decisions+changes | bul=2.2.K6,2.1.K15,3.3.K7
C: To increase write availability and protect recent updates against failure, AWS recommends a Multi-AZ deployment rather than read replicas, because read replica writes happen after the source and replication lag can vary significantly.
Q: Should you use a read replica or Multi-AZ to protect recent writes against failure?
V: v1=run your DB instance as a Multi-AZ deployment || lag=this replication “lag” can vary significantly
S: scope='Multi-AZ DB instance deployments' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/rds/faqs] If you are looking to use replication to increase database write availability and protect recent database updates against various failure conditions, we recommend you run your DB instance as a Multi-AZ deployment.
E: [www/rds/faqs] With Amazon RDS Read Replicas, which employ supported engines' native, asynchronous replication, database writes occur on a read replica after they have already occurred on the source DB instance, and this replication “lag” can vary significantly.
=== rds-faq-multiaz-automatic-failover | rds | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-26-faq | bul=2.2.K6
C: During some planned maintenance, or on DB instance or Availability Zone failure, RDS fails over to the standby automatically, and because the DB instance name record stays the same, the application resumes without manual intervention.
Q: When does a Multi-AZ RDS DB instance fail over, and does the application need to change its endpoint?
V: automatically-failover-to-the-standby=automatically failover to the standby || name-record-for-your-db-instance=name record for your DB instance
S: scope='Multi-AZ DB instance deployments' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/rds/faqs] During certain types of planned maintenance, or in the unlikely event of DB instance failure or Availability Zone failure, Amazon RDS will automatically failover to the standby so that you can resume database writes and reads as soon as the standby is promoted. Since the name record for your DB instance remains the same, your application can resume database operation without the need for manual administrative intervention.
=== rds-faq-multiaz-backup-storage-same | rds | pricing-dimension | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-26-faq | bul=4.3.S2,2.2.K6

E: [www/rds/faqs] Backup Storage – Your backup storage usage will not change whether your DB instance is a standard or Multi-AZ deployment. Backups will simply be taken from your standby to avoid I/O suspension on the DB instance primary.

=== rds-faq-multiaz-replication-synchronous | rds | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-26-faq | bul=2.2.K6,3.3.K7
C: Multi-AZ replication is synchronous: database writes are concurrent on the primary and standby, which protects the latest updates in a failover.
Q: How does Multi-AZ replication differ from read replica replication?
V: concurrent-on-the-primary-and-standby=concurrent on the primary and standby
S: scope='Multi-AZ DB instance deployments' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/rds/faqs] In contrast, the replication used by Multi-AZ deployments is synchronous, meaning that all database writes are concurrent on the primary and standby.
=== rds-faq-multiaz-standby-no-reads | rds | restriction | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-26-faq | bul=2.2.K6,3.3.K7
C: In a Multi-AZ DB instance deployment, the standby cannot serve read requests; Multi-AZ is for availability and durability, not read scaling.
Q: Can you read from the standby of a Multi-AZ RDS DB instance deployment?
V: a-multi-az-standby-cannot-serve-read-requests=a Multi-AZ standby cannot serve read requests
S: scope='Multi-AZ DB instance deployments' dom='' adj=False cond='Multi-AZ DB instance deployment (one standby)'
E: [www/rds/faqs] No, a Multi-AZ standby cannot serve read requests. Multi-AZ deployments are designed to provide enhanced database availability and durability, rather than read scaling benefits.

=== rds-major-vs-minor-upgrade | rds | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=3.3.S2
C: A major engine version upgrade can bring changes that are not compatible with existing applications and must be started manually; a minor version upgrade is backward-compatible and can be applied manually or with Auto minor version upgrade.
Q: How do major and minor RDS engine upgrades differ?
V: v1=not compatible with existing applications || v2=backward-compatible || v3=**Auto minor version upgrade**
S: scope='DB engine version upgrades' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/USER_UpgradeDBInstance.Upgrading] In general, a *major engine version upgrade* can introduce changes that are not compatible with existing applications. In contrast, a *minor version upgrade* includes only changes that are backward-compatible with existing applications.
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/USER_UpgradeDBInstance.Upgrading] For major version upgrades, you must manually modify the DB engine version through the AWS Management Console, AWS CLI, or RDS API. For minor version upgrades, you can manually modify the engine version, or you can choose to enable the **Auto minor version upgrade** option.

=== rds-max-connections-from-instance-class | rds | default | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=3.3.K5,3.3.K4
C: If you do not set the connection limit (such as max_connections) in the parameter group, Amazon RDS calculates it automatically from the DB instance class, so it can change when you scale the instance.
Q: What decides max_connections on an RDS DB instance by default?
V: v1=based on the database instance class || v2=can change when you scale your database instances
S: scope='max_connections parameter' dom='default' adj=True cond='not set explicitly in the parameter group'
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/rds-proxy-best-practices.configuration] If you did not explicitly set the connection limit (such as the `max_connections` parameter) in your database's parameter group, Amazon RDS and Aurora calculate it automatically based on the database instance class. As a result, the implicit connection limit can change when you scale your database instances, thus influencing the maximum pool size in the proxy.

=== rds-multi-az-billing-write-io-doubles | rds | pricing-dimension | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=2.2.K6,4.3.S2
C: A Multi-AZ DB instance deployment is billed at Multi-AZ pricing; write I/O usage doubles because data is synchronously replicated to the standby, read I/O stays the same, and replication data transfer between primary and standby is not charged.
Q: How does a Multi-AZ DB instance deployment change the RDS bill?
V: v1=Multi-AZ pricing || v2=Write I/O usage associated with database updates || v3=not charged for the data transfer || read=Read I/O usage will remain the same
S: scope='Multi-AZ DB instance deployments' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/rds/faqs] If you specify that your DB instance should be a Multi-AZ deployment, you will be billed according to the Multi-AZ pricing posted on the Amazon RDS pricing page.
E: [www/rds/faqs] Write I/O usage associated with database updates will double as Amazon RDS synchronously replicates your data to the standby DB instance. Read I/O usage will remain the same.
E: [www/rds/faqs] Data transfer – You are not charged for the data transfer incurred in replicating data between your primary and standby.

=== rds-multi-az-cluster-instance-read-replicas | rds | restriction | hard=True | extended | frequently-changing | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=3.3.K7,3.3.S1

Currently, you can't create a Multi-AZ DB cluster read replica from an existing Multi-AZ DB cluster.
=== rds-multi-az-cluster-native-replication-failover-target | rds | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=2.2.K6
C: In a Multi-AZ DB cluster, reader DB instances are automatic failover targets and also serve read traffic; on a writer outage RDS fails over to the reader with the most recent change record.
Q: Which instance becomes the writer when a Multi-AZ DB cluster fails over?
V: v1=act as automatic failover targets || v2=has the most recent change record
S: scope='Multi-AZ DB cluster deployment' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/multi-az-db-clusters-concepts] Reader DB instances act as automatic failover targets and also serve read traffic to increase application read throughput. If an outage occurs on your writer DB instance, RDS manages failover to one of the reader DB instances. RDS does this based on which reader DB instance has the most recent change record.

E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/multi-az-db-clusters-concepts] Multi-AZ DB cluster deployments use semisynchronous replication, which requires acknowledgment from at least one reader DB instance in order for a change to be committed.

=== rds-multi-az-cname-flip-retry | rds | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=2.2.K6
C: During failover RDS flips the CNAME record of the DB instance to the standby, which is promoted to primary; AWS encourages connection retry logic in the application.

S: scope='Multi-AZ DB instance deployments' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/rds/faqs] When failing over, Amazon RDS simply flips the canonical name record (CNAME) for your DB instance to point at the standby, which is in turn promoted to become the new primary. We encourage you to follow best practices and implement database connection retry at the application layer.

E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/Concepts.MultiAZ.Migrating] Instead, create a read replica, enable backups on it, convert it to Multi-AZ, load data into its volumes, and then promote it to the primary DB instance.

=== rds-multi-az-conversion-no-downtime-perf-impact | rds | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=2.2.S2

S: scope='Single-AZ to Multi-AZ DB instance conversion' dom='' adj=False cond=''

=== rds-multi-az-conversion-snapshot-steps | rds | behavior | hard=False | extended | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=2.2.S2
C: When a Single-AZ DB instance is modified to Multi-AZ, RDS takes a snapshot of the primary's EBS volumes, creates the standby's volumes from that snapshot, and turns on synchronous block-level replication.
Q: How does RDS build the standby when you convert a Single-AZ DB instance to Multi-AZ?
V: v1=Takes a snapshot of the primary DB instance's || v2=synchronous block-level replication
S: scope='Single-AZ to Multi-AZ DB instance conversion' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/Concepts.MultiAZ.Migrating] Takes a snapshot of the primary DB instance's Amazon Elastic Block Store (EBS) volumes.
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/Concepts.MultiAZ.Migrating] Creates new volumes for the standby replica from the snapshot. These volumes initialize in the background, and maximum volume performance is achieved after the data is fully initialized.
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/Concepts.MultiAZ.Migrating] Turns on synchronous block-level replication between the volumes of the primary and standby replicas.
=== rds-multi-az-convert-by-modifying | rds | capability | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=2.2.S2

=== rds-multi-az-durability-no-restore-needed | rds | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=2.2.K6
C: If a primary storage volume fails in a Multi-AZ deployment, RDS fails over to the standby with all updates intact, whereas a Single-AZ deployment needs a user-initiated restore and loses updates after the latest restorable time.
Q: Why does Multi-AZ give better data durability than Single-AZ?
V: v1=all of your database updates are intact || v2=a user-initiated restore operation would be required
S: scope='Multi-AZ DB instance deployments' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/rds/faqs] For example, if a storage volume on your primary fails, Amazon RDS automatically initiates a failover to the standby, where all of your database updates are intact. This provides additional data durability relative to standard deployments in a single AZ, where a user-initiated restore operation would be required and updates that occurred after the latest restorable time (typically within the last five minutes) would not be available.
=== rds-multi-az-engine-upgrade-both-down | rds | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=2.2.K6
C: An engine version upgrade of a Multi-AZ DB instance modifies the primary and the standby at the same time, so both are unavailable and there is downtime until the upgrade completes.
Q: Does Multi-AZ avoid downtime during a database engine upgrade?
V: v1=modifies both primary and secondary DB instances || v2=causes downtime until the upgrade is complete
S: scope='Multi-AZ DB instance deployments, engine version upgrades' dom='' adj=False cond='RDS for SQL Server uses rolling upgrades'
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/USER_UpgradeDBInstance.Maintenance] If you upgrade the database engine for your DB instance in a Multi-AZ deployment, Amazon RDS modifies both primary and secondary DB instances at the same time. In this case, both the primary and secondary DB instances in the Multi-AZ deployment are unavailable during the upgrade. This operation causes downtime until the upgrade is complete.
=== rds-multi-az-failover-dns-change-reconnect | rds | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=2.2.K6
C: At failover RDS changes the DNS record of the DB instance to point to the standby, so existing connections to the DB instance must be re-established.
Q: What happens to the endpoint and to open connections when a Multi-AZ DB instance fails over?
V: v1=changes the Domain Name System (DNS) record || v2=re-establish any existing connections
S: scope='Multi-AZ DB instance deployment' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/Concepts.MultiAZ.Failover] The failover mechanism automatically changes the Domain Name System (DNS) record of the DB instance to point to the standby DB instance. As a result, you need to re-establish any existing connections to your DB instance.

=== rds-multi-az-one-vs-two-standbys | rds | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=2.2.K6,2.2.S2
C: A Multi-AZ DB instance deployment has one standby that provides failover support but serves no read traffic; a Multi-AZ DB cluster deployment has two standbys that provide failover support and can also serve read traffic.

When the deployment has one standby DB instance, it's called a *Multi-AZ DB instance deployment*. A Multi-AZ DB instance deployment has one standby DB instance that provides failover support, but doesn't serve read traffic.
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/Concepts.MultiAZ] When the deployment has two standby DB instances, it's called a *Multi-AZ DB cluster deployment*. A Multi-AZ DB cluster deployment has standby DB instances that provide failover support and can also serve read traffic.

=== rds-parameter-group-default-not-modifiable | rds | restriction | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=3.3.S2

S: scope='DB parameter groups' dom='' adj=False cond=''

E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/rpg-delayed-replication] This provides valuable protection against data corruption, accidental data loss, or erroneous transactions that could otherwise be immediately propagated to all replicas.

E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/rds-proxy.howitworks] **Standard IAM authentication**: Enforce IAM authentication for connections to your proxy while the proxy connects to the database using credentials stored in Secrets Manager.

=== rds-proxy-multi-user-fragments-pool | rds-proxy | behavior | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=3.3.K5
C: Back-end connections are reused only for clients with the same database user name, so many unique database accounts (for example one per tenant) fragment the pool into micro-pools and reduce RDS Proxy's efficiency.

=== rds-proxy-multiplexing-default | rds-proxy | default | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=3.3.K5
C: By default RDS Proxy can reuse a connection after each transaction in a session (multiplexing); taking a connection from the pool to reuse it is called borrowing.

S: scope='RDS Proxy connections' dom='default' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/rds-proxy.howitworks] By default, RDS Proxy can reuse a connection after each transaction in your session. This transaction-level reuse is called *multiplexing*. When RDS Proxy temporarily removes a connection from the connection pool to reuse it, that operation is called *borrowing* the connection.

E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/rds-proxy.howitworks] You can open many simultaneous connections to the proxy, and the proxy keeps a smaller number of connections open to the DB instance or cluster. Doing so further minimizes the memory overhead for connections on the database server. This technique also reduces the chance of "too many connections" errors.

=== rds-proxy-pinned-until-session-ends | rds-proxy | behavior | hard=False | extended | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=3.3.K5
C: When a connection is pinned, each later transaction uses the same database connection until the session ends, and no other client connection can reuse that database connection meanwhile.
Q: How long does RDS Proxy pinning last?
V: v1=until the session ends
S: scope='Amazon RDS Proxy' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/rds-proxy-pinning] When a connection is pinned, each later transaction uses the same underlying database connection until the session ends. Other client connections also can't reuse that database connection until the session ends. The session ends when the client connection is dropped.
=== rds-proxy-pinning-definition | rds-proxy | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=3.3.K5
C: When RDS Proxy cannot be sure that reusing a connection outside the current session is safe, it keeps the session on the same connection until the session ends; this is called pinning.
Q: What is session pinning in RDS Proxy?
V: v1=This fallback behavior is called *pinning*. || v2=keeps the session on the same connection
S: scope='Amazon RDS Proxy' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/rds-proxy.howitworks] In some cases, RDS Proxy can't be sure that it's safe to reuse a database connection outside of the current session. In these cases, it keeps the session on the same connection until the session ends. This fallback behavior is called *pinning*.
=== rds-proxy-pinning-minimize-init-query | rds-proxy | behavior | hard=False | extended | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=3.3.K5
C: To minimize pinning, set variables consistently across connections, move identical SET statements into the proxy's initialization query, and watch the CloudWatch metric DatabaseConnectionsCurrentlySessionPinned; for PostgreSQL, setting a variable leads to session pinning.
Q: How do you reduce pinning in RDS Proxy and see how often it happens?

S: scope='Amazon RDS Proxy' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/rds-proxy-pinning] See how frequently pinning occurs by monitoring the Amazon CloudWatch metric `DatabaseConnectionsCurrentlySessionPinned`.
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/rds-proxy-pinning] However, for PostgreSQL setting a variable leads to session pinning.
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/rds-proxy-pinning] In this case, you move the statements that set up the initial session state into the initialization query used by a proxy.
=== rds-proxy-pinning-one-to-one | rds-proxy | behavior | hard=False | extended | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=3.3.K5
C: Pinning creates an exclusive one-to-one association between a client-proxy connection and a proxy-database connection, so avoiding it matters whenever RDS Proxy is used mainly for scalability and efficiency.
Q: Why does heavy pinning remove the benefit of RDS Proxy?
V: v1=exclusive 1:1 association
S: scope='Amazon RDS Proxy' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/rds-proxy-best-practices.workload-considerations] In other words, pinning creates an exclusive 1:1 association between a client-proxy connection and a proxy-database connection. Avoiding pinning is important in all scenarios where RDS Proxy is used mainly for scalability and efficiency reasons.
=== rds-proxy-pinning-reduces-reuse | rds-proxy | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=3.3.K5
C: RDS Proxy pins a client connection to one database connection when it detects a session state change that is not appropriate for other sessions, such as changing a session variable; pinning reduces the effectiveness of connection reuse.
Q: What causes RDS Proxy session pinning, and why does it matter?
V: v1=Pinning reduces the effectiveness of connection reuse. || v2=session state change
S: scope='Amazon RDS Proxy' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/rds-proxy-pinning] RDS Proxy automatically pins a client connection to a specific DB connection when it detects a session state change that isn't appropriate for other sessions. Pinning reduces the effectiveness of connection reuse.
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/rds-proxy-pinning] Thus, when RDS Proxy processes requests to change session variables or configuration settings, it pins that session to the DB connection.
=== rds-proxy-pinning-statement-size | rds-proxy | limit | hard=True | reference | stable | by=mine-26-numbers | bul=3.3.K5

E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/rds-proxy] Any statement with a text size greater than 16 KB causes the proxy to pin the session to the current connection.
=== rds-proxy-pricing-endpoints | rds-proxy | pricing-dimension | hard=True | reference | stable | by=mine-26-decisions+changes | bul=4.3.S2

E: [www/rds/proxy/pricing] Adding a RDS Proxy endpoint provisions an AWS PrivateLink interface endpoint, which incurs additional charges as described on the PrivateLink pricing page.

E: [www/rds/proxy/pricing] Partial hours are billed in 1-second increments with a 10-minute minimum charge following a billable status change such as creating, starting, or modifying.

=== rds-proxy-read-only-endpoint-clusters | rds-proxy | restriction | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=2.2.K9,3.3.K5
C: The default proxy endpoint for a Multi-AZ DB cluster is read/write and sends all requests to the writer; only for a proxy associated with a Multi-AZ DB cluster can you add read/write or read-only endpoints, and a read-only endpoint spreads read queries over the readers.
Q: Can RDS Proxy provide a read-only endpoint, and for which deployments?
V: v1=additional read/write or read-only endpoints || v2=sends all requests to the writer instance
S: scope='RDS Proxy with Multi-AZ DB clusters' dom='' adj=False cond='the proxy is associated with a Multi-AZ DB cluster'
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/rds-proxy-endpoints] By default, the endpoint that you connect to when you use RDS Proxy with a Multi-AZ DB cluster has read/write capability. As a result, this endpoint sends all requests to the writer instance of the cluster. All of those connections count against the `max_connections` value for the writer instance. If your proxy is associated with a Multi-AZ DB cluster, then you can create additional read/write or read-only endpoints for that proxy.
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/rds-proxy.howitworks] For RDS DB clusters, you can also create additional read/write and read-only endpoints.

=== rds-proxy-surges-queue-throttle | rds-proxy | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=3.3.K5,4.3.K6
C: RDS Proxy queues or throttles application connections that cannot be served at once from the pool, so latency may rise but the application keeps scaling; requests beyond your limits are rejected (load shedding).
Q: What does RDS Proxy do with connection requests during an unpredictable traffic surge?
V: v1=queues or throttles application connections || v2=it sheds load
S: scope='Amazon RDS Proxy' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/rds-proxy] RDS Proxy queues or throttles application connections that can't be served immediately from the connection pool. Although latencies might increase, your application can continue to scale without abruptly failing or overwhelming the database. If connection requests exceed the limits you specify, RDS Proxy rejects application connections (that is, it sheds load).
=== rds-proxy-tls-acm-no-cert-download | rds-proxy | behavior | hard=False | extended | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=2.2.K9,1.3.S2
C: RDS Proxy uses certificates from AWS Certificate Manager, so applications that connect through it do not need to download RDS certificates; clients can use a higher TLS version to the proxy than the database supports.
Q: Do applications using RDS Proxy need the RDS certificate bundle?
V: v1=AWS Certificate Manager (ACM) || v2=don't need to download Amazon RDS certificates
S: scope='Amazon RDS Proxy' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/rds-proxy.howitworks] RDS Proxy uses certificates from the AWS Certificate Manager (ACM). If you are using RDS Proxy, you don't need to download Amazon RDS certificates or update applications that use RDS Proxy connections.
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/rds-proxy.howitworks] You can connect to the proxy using a higher version of TLS than you use in the underlying database.
=== rds-proxy-vpc-two-subnets-two-azs | rds-proxy | restriction | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=2.2.K9

V: v1=a minimum of two subnets || v2=in different Availability Zones
S: scope='RDS Proxy VPC' dom='min' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/rds-proxy-network-prereqs] Using RDS Proxy requires you to have a common virtual private cloud (VPC) between your RDS DB instance and RDS Proxy. This VPC should have a minimum of two subnets that are in different Availability Zones.
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/rds-proxy-network-prereqs] Your client application resources such as Amazon EC2, Lambda, or Amazon ECS can be in the same VPC as the proxy. Or they can be in a separate VPC from the proxy.

=== rds-public-access-option | rds | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=1.3.S2
C: A DB instance in a VPC always has a private IP address; the Public access option decides whether it also gets a public IP address, and public access still needs security group inbound rules and an internet gateway on the DB subnet group's subnets.

S: scope='DB instances in a VPC' dom='' adj=False cond=''

E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/USER_VPC.WorkingWithRDSInstanceinaVPC] That public access is not permitted if the security group assigned to the DB instance doesn't include inbound rules that permit it. In addition, for a DB instance to be publicly accessible, the subnets in its DB subnet group must have an internet gateway.

=== rds-read-replica-create-io-suspension | rds | behavior | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=3.3.S1
C: Creating a read replica causes a very brief I/O suspension (typically about one second) on the source when the snapshot begins; a Multi-AZ source avoids it because the snapshot is taken from the secondary.
Q: Does creating a read replica interrupt the source DB instance?
V: v1=about one second || v2=snapshot is taken from the secondary DB instance
S: scope='source DB instance when a read replica is created' dom='' adj=False cond='avoided when the source is a Multi-AZ deployment'
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/USER_ReadRepl.Create] The source DB instance experiences a very brief I/O suspension when the DB snapshot operation begins. The I/O suspension typically lasts about one second. You can avoid the I/O suspension if the source DB instance is a Multi-AZ deployment, because in that case the snapshot is taken from the secondary DB instance.
=== rds-read-replica-created-from-snapshot | rds | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=3.3.K7
C: When you create a read replica, RDS takes a snapshot of the source instance, creates a read-only instance from it, and then updates it with the engine's asynchronous replication whenever the primary changes.
Q: How does RDS create and then update a read replica?
V: v1=takes a snapshot of the source instance || v2=asynchronous replication method
S: scope='RDS DB instance read replicas' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/USER_ReadRepl] Then Amazon RDS takes a snapshot of the source instance and creates a read-only instance from the snapshot. Amazon RDS then uses the asynchronous replication method for the DB engine to update the read replica whenever there is a change to the primary DB instance.
=== rds-read-replica-cross-region-secure-channel | rds | behavior | hard=False | extended | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=3.3.K7

=== rds-read-replica-definition | rds | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=2.1.K15,3.3.K7
C: A read replica is a read-only copy of a DB instance; routing queries to it reduces the load on the primary and lets read-heavy workloads scale out beyond one DB instance.
Q: What is an RDS read replica and what is it for?
V: v1=read-only copy of a DB instance || v2=read-heavy database workloads
S: scope='RDS DB instance read replicas' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/USER_ReadRepl] A read replica is a read-only copy of a DB instance. You can reduce the load on your primary DB instance by routing queries from your applications to the read replica. In this way, you can elastically scale out beyond the capacity constraints of a single DB instance for read-heavy database workloads.

=== rds-read-replica-promote-reboot-minutes | rds | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=3.3.S1,2.2.K6

E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/USER_ReadRepl.Promote] When you promote a read replica, RDS stops replication and reboots the read replica. When the reboot is complete, the read replica is available as a new DB instance.
=== rds-read-replica-promote-recovery-steps | rds | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=2.2.K6

E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/USER_ReadRepl.Promote] If you are aware of the ramifications and limitations of asynchronous replication and you still want to use read replica promotion for data recovery, you can.

=== rds-read-replica-read-only-connections | rds | restriction | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=3.3.K7

A standby replica and mounted replica don't accept user connections and so can't serve read-only workloads.

=== rds-replica-lag-causes-faq | rds | behavior | hard=False | extended | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=3.3.K7
C: A read replica can fall behind when write I/O on the source exceeds the replica's apply rate (more likely if the replica has less compute), when complex or long-running transactions hold up replication, or because of network partitions or latency.

E: [www/rds/faqs] Complex or long-running transactions to the source DB Instance hold up replication to the read replica
E: [www/rds/faqs] Network partitions or latency between the source DB instance and a read replica
=== rds-replica-lag-metric-definition | rds | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=3.3.K7
C: Replica lag is the time difference between the latest transaction on the writer and the latest applied transaction on a reader; the CloudWatch metric ReplicaLag represents it.

S: scope='RDS DB instance read replicas' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/multi-az-db-clusters-concepts] *Replica lag* is the difference in time between the latest transaction on the writer DB instance and the latest applied transaction on a reader DB instance. The Amazon CloudWatch metric `ReplicaLag` represents this time difference.

=== rds-restore-lazy-loading | rds | behavior | hard=False | extended | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=4.3.S1
C: A restored DB instance can be used as soon as it is available while it keeps loading data from Amazon S3 in the background (lazy loading); data not yet loaded is downloaded on first access.
Q: Why can a freshly restored RDS DB instance be slow at first?
V: v1=lazy loading || v2=continues to load data in the background
S: scope='restoring a DB instance from a snapshot or a point in time' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/USER_RestoreFromSnapshot] You can use the restored DB instance as soon as its status is `available`. The DB instance continues to load data in the background. This is known as *lazy loading*. If you access data that hasn't been loaded yet, the DB instance immediately downloads the requested data from Amazon S3, and then continues loading the rest of the data in the background.

=== rds-snapshot-single-az-io-suspension | rds | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=4.3.S1,2.2.K6
C: A DB snapshot of a Single-AZ DB instance causes a brief I/O suspension of a few seconds to a few minutes; for Multi-AZ MariaDB, MySQL, Oracle and PostgreSQL the backup is taken from the standby so the primary's I/O is not suspended, while SQL Server Multi-AZ still suspends I/O briefly.
Q: Does taking an RDS snapshot interrupt I/O, and how does Multi-AZ change that?
V: v1=brief I/O suspension || v2=the backup is taken from the standby || v3=For SQL Server, I/O activity is suspended briefly
S: scope='DB snapshots and backups, per deployment and engine' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/USER_CreateSnapshot] Creating this DB snapshot on a Single-AZ DB instance results in a brief I/O suspension that can last from a few seconds to a few minutes, depending on the size and class of your DB instance. For MariaDB, MySQL, Oracle, and PostgreSQL, I/O activity is not suspended on your primary during backup for Multi-AZ deployments, because the backup is taken from the standby. For SQL Server, I/O activity is suspended briefly during backup for Multi-AZ deployments.

=== rds-snapshots-incremental | rds | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=4.3.S1

S: scope='DB snapshots' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/USER_WorkingWithAutomatedBackups] The first snapshot of a DB instance contains the data for the full database. Subsequent snapshots of the same database are incremental, which means that only the data that has changed after your most recent snapshot is saved.

=== rds-wa-cross-region-primary-replica | rds | behavior | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-26-decisions+changes | bul=2.2.S2
C: For multi-Region designs, Well-Architected notes that with Amazon RDS (unlike DynamoDB global tables) you designate a primary read/write Region and read-only replica Regions.
Q: How do Amazon RDS multi-Region replicas differ from DynamoDB global tables?
V: v1=designate a primary (read/write) Region
S: scope='AWS Well-Architected Framework guidance' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/wellarchitected/latest/reliability-pillar/rel_fault_isolation_multiaz_region_system] With other AWS-managed data services, such as Amazon RDS, Amazon Aurora, and Amazon Elasticache, you designate a primary (read/write) Region and replica (read-only) Regions.
=== rds-wa-cross-region-replication-primary-writes | rds | behavior | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-26-behavior | bul=2.2.S2
C: The Well-Architected reliability pillar lists Amazon RDS among managed data services with cross-Region replication; unlike DynamoDB global tables, such services need a primary Region for writes while other Regions hold read-only replicas.
Q: In a multi-Region design, where must writes go for Amazon RDS?
V: v1=designate a primary Region for writes || v2=other Regions contain read-only replicas
S: scope='multi-Region workloads' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/wellarchitected/latest/reliability-pillar/rel_fault_isolation_multiaz_region_system] Many AWS managed data services provide cross-Regional replication capability, including Amazon S3, Amazon DynamoDB, Amazon RDS, Amazon Aurora, Amazon Redshift, Amazon Elasticache, and Amazon EFS.
E: [docs/wellarchitected/latest/reliability-pillar/rel_fault_isolation_multiaz_region_system] With other services, you must designate a primary Region for writes, as other Regions contain read-only replicas.
=== rds-wa-managed-data-enable-cross-az | rds | behavior | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-26-decisions+changes | bul=2.2.S2,2.2.K6
C: Well-Architected REL10-BP01 advises, for managed data services such as Amazon RDS, to study the service's replication and resilience capabilities and enable cross-AZ replication and failover if necessary.
Q: What does REL10-BP01 advise for an Amazon RDS database?
V: v1=Enable cross-AZ replication and failover if necessary
S: scope='AWS Well-Architected Framework guidance' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/wellarchitected/latest/reliability-pillar/rel_fault_isolation_multiaz_region_system] If you use an AWS-managed data service such as Amazon RDS, Amazon ElastiCache, or Amazon FSx, study its user guide to understand its data replication and resilience capabilities.
E: [docs/wellarchitected/latest/reliability-pillar/rel_fault_isolation_multiaz_region_system] Enable cross-AZ replication and failover if necessary.
=== rds-wa-purpose-built-anti-pattern | rds | behavior | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-26-decisions+changes | bul=3.3.S2
C: The Well-Architected performance pillar lists as an anti-pattern sticking to one data store because the team already knows one particular type of database solution.
Q: What data store anti-pattern does the Well-Architected performance pillar name?
V: v1=You stick to one data store
S: scope='AWS Well-Architected Framework guidance' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/wellarchitected/latest/performance-efficiency-pillar/perf_data_use_purpose_built_data_store] You stick to one data store because there is internal experience and knowledge of one particular type of database solution.
=== rds-wa-relational-acid | rds | behavior | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-26-decisions+changes | bul=3.3.S2,3.3.S3
C: Well-Architected describes relational databases (Aurora, RDS, Redshift) as designed for ACID transactions, referential integrity and strong data consistency, used by many traditional applications, ERP, CRM and ecommerce.
Q: What does the Well-Architected performance pillar say relational databases are designed for?
V: v1=referential integrity and strong data consistency
S: scope='AWS Well-Architected Framework guidance' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/wellarchitected/latest/performance-efficiency-pillar/perf_data_use_purpose_built_data_store] Designed to support ACID (atomicity, consistency, isolation, durability) transactions, and maintain referential integrity and strong data consistency.
=== rds-wa-relational-storage-upper-bounds | rds | behavior | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-26-decisions+changes | bul=3.3.S2
C: Well-Architected notes that relational databases have upper bounds on provisioned storage and often must be horizontally partitioned (sharded) when they reach these limits, while serverless databases such as DynamoDB scale dynamically.
Q: How do relational databases and serverless databases differ when storage requirements grow?
V: v1=upper bounds on provisioned storage
S: scope='AWS Well-Architected Framework guidance' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/wellarchitected/latest/performance-efficiency-pillar/perf_data_use_purpose_built_data_store] Relational databases have upper bounds on provisioned storage, and often must be horizontally partitioned using mechanisms such as sharding once they reach these limits.
=== rds-zero-etl-what | rds | capability | hard=True | extended | frequently-changing | by=mine-26-decisions+changes | bul=3.3.S2
C: An RDS zero-ETL integration is a fully managed way to make transactional data from an RDS database available in Amazon Redshift or an Amazon SageMaker AI lakehouse in near real time, for analytics and machine learning.
Q: What does an Amazon RDS zero-ETL integration do?
V: v1=near real-time analytics and machine learning
S: scope='RDS zero-ETL integrations' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/zero-etl] An Amazon RDS zero-ETL integration with Amazon Redshift and Amazon SageMaker AI enables near real-time analytics and machine learning (ML) using data from RDS.
E: [docs/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/zero-etl] It's a fully managed solution for making transactional data available in your analytics destination after it is written to an RDS database.
